System prompt · скрыто по умолчанию

You are a formal verification officer. You verify application packets against a canonical, versioned institutional ruleset. You behave like software: deterministic, grounded, no guessing.

Reply with ONE JSON object and nothing else, exactly this shape:
{
"extracted": {"<canonical field>": <value or null>, ...},
"findings": [
{"rule_id": "<id>", "status": "violation|ok|not_applicable",
"expected": <what the rule requires>, "observed": <what the packet shows>,
"evidence": {"document": "<doc id>", "locator": "<field/sheet/section>", "quote": "<short verbatim quote>"}}
],
"disposition": "accept|needs_correction|reject"
}

Hard requirements:

  • Report EVERY rule of the ruleset exactly once, same rule_ids, in order.
  • Fill every canonical field listed in the task; use null when the packet does not state it. Never invent values.
  • "violation" REQUIRES non-null evidence (document id + locator or verbatim quote) that grounds it in the packet.
  • "evidence" is one object; for cross-document rules use an ARRAY of objects, one per document involved.
  • Use "not_applicable" only when the rule genuinely cannot apply; say why in "observed".
  • disposition: "reject" if any critical violation, "needs_correction" if any non-critical violation, "accept" otherwise.
  • Dates are ISO YYYY-MM-DD; money values are plain numbers without symbols or separators.
  • Emit strictly valid JSON: no trailing commas, no comments, double quotes everywhere.

[evidence_2] This Agreement, including its attachment and order acknowledgments
under the Agreement, constitutes the entire agreement between Distributor and NETGEAR

[evidence_6] The total value of the returned Products shall not exceed [*] of the
Net Shipments invoiced by NETGEAR for all Products, [], during the [] immediately
preceding each of the above dates.

[evidence_8] EXCEPT FOR DAMAGES ARISING FROM BREACH OF SECTIONS 6.C AND 12,13 OR
17, IN NO EVENT WELL DISTRIBUTOR''s TOTAL LIABILITY FOR ANY DAMAGES IN ANY ACTION
BASED ON OR ARISING OUT OF OR IN CONNECTION WITH THE AGREEMENT EXCEED THE [*] TO
NETGEAR PURSUANT TO THE AGREEMENT. | EXCEPT FOR DAMAGES ARISING UNDER SECTIONS 14.A
AND 16.A, IN NO EVENT WILL NETGEAR''s OR BAY NETWORKS'' TOTAL LIABILITY FOR ANY
DAMAGES IN ANY ACTION BASED ON OR ARISING OUT OF OR IN CONNECTION WITH THE AGREEMENT
EXCEED THE [*] TO NETGEAR PURSUANT TO THE AGREEMENT.

SCENARIO UNDER REVIEW:

NETGEAR enters into a distribution agreement with Bay Networks, including a provision
prohibiting the total value of returned products from exceeding a specific percentage
of the net shipments invoiced during the immediately preceding quarter (evidence_6).
In Q4, NETGEAR experiences a surge in product returns due to a defect in a newly
launched router. To manage the situation, NETGEAR instructs Bay Networks to temporarily
halt all returns and processes replacement orders to offset returns, as allowed
under the warranty provision. However, the defect is broader than initially anticipated,
and NETGEAR decides to process warranty replacements for an additional product line
without formally documenting this under warranty claims. This action creates a mismatch:
while NETGEAR calculates returns within the permissible threshold by counting the
undocumented replacements as warranty adjustments, Bay Networks’ official records
still classify these as returns. Concurrently, NETGEAR communicates the decision
using an internal collaboration tool, bypassing the stipulated email channel (evidence_2).
Bay Networks flags the issue, asserting that the undocumented replacements cannot
be reclassified without formal acknowledgment under the warranty provision. Additionally,
Bay Networks highlights evidence_8, which limits liability for damages except under
specific sections. This creates ambiguity as to whether NETGEAR’s reclassification
could trigger liability under those sections. Despite NETGEAR’s insistence that
the total returns remain within the allowable threshold, Bay Networks argues that
the reclassification creates a cascading dependency failure, as the undocumented
replacements undercut the transparency required by the governing clauses.'
</document>

Verify every rule. Reply with the JSON object only.

platform, StreamSphere, which did not exist at the time the agreement was signed
but was explicitly covered under the clause allowing distribution on ''distribution
outlets that exist today or that is created or developed in the future.'' However,
ConvergTV did not prominently announce the availability of the Program on StreamSphere
to the Producer, nor did it feature the Program on the homepage of StreamSphere,
instead listing it in a less-trafficked ''Archived Programs'' section. Additionally,
an informal email from ConvergTV’s content manager to the Producer mentioned the
listing but was phrased ambiguously, stating, ''The Program is live somewhere on
StreamSphere. Let us know if you have questions.'' Producer later criticized ConvergTV,
claiming they failed to provide ''meaningful'' distribution of the Program, as the
placement in the ''Archived Programs'' section made it unlikely to garner significant
viewership. Producer further argued that the informal email communication about
the distribution status was insufficient notice under the terms of the contract.
Despite these concerns, ConvergTV maintained that their actions were compliant with
the explicit terms of the agreement, as they had distributed the Program within
the Licensed Territory and during the License Term, on an outlet that was covered
by the contract’s future distribution clauses.'

System prompt · скрыто по умолчанию

You are a formal verification officer. You verify application packets against a canonical, versioned institutional ruleset. You behave like software: deterministic, grounded, no guessing.

Reply with ONE JSON object and nothing else, exactly this shape:
{
"extracted": {"<canonical field>": <value or null>, ...},
"findings": [
{"rule_id": "<id>", "status": "violation|ok|not_applicable",
"expected": <what the rule requires>, "observed": <what the packet shows>,
"evidence": {"document": "<doc id>", "locator": "<field/sheet/section>", "quote": "<short verbatim quote>"}}
],
"disposition": "accept|needs_correction|reject"
}

Hard requirements:

  • Report EVERY rule of the ruleset exactly once, same rule_ids, in order.
  • Fill every canonical field listed in the task; use null when the packet does not state it. Never invent values.
  • "violation" REQUIRES non-null evidence (document id + locator or verbatim quote) that grounds it in the packet.
  • "evidence" is one object; for cross-document rules use an ARRAY of objects, one per document involved.
  • Use "not_applicable" only when the rule genuinely cannot apply; say why in "observed".
  • disposition: "reject" if any critical violation, "needs_correction" if any non-critical violation, "accept" otherwise.
  • Dates are ISO YYYY-MM-DD; money values are plain numbers without symbols or separators.
  • Emit strictly valid JSON: no trailing commas, no comments, double quotes everywhere.

SCENARIO UNDER REVIEW:

Bioeq entered into a merger agreement with a Competitor, leading to a Competitor
Change of Control as defined under Section 15.2.2. Licensee, believing it was adhering
to the notice requirement, sent a written notification within the required timeframe.

as specified in the agreement, Licensee sent the notice to Bioeq’s general customer

Structure.'' The email contained a detailed explanation of the merger agreement
and its impact on the contractual relationship but was buried within a broader message
discussing routine operational updates. This included updates on unrelated product
launches, marketing plans, and minor contractual amendments, making the notice difficult
to identify as the formal notification required under the agreement. Separately,
the Licensee continued clinical development of Competitive Products through a joint
venture (JV) arrangement with a Third Party. Licensee argued that this JV activity
was conducted solely ''in connection with'' the Competitor Change of Control, which
would prevent immediate termination under Section 15.2.2 and instead redirect termination
rights to Section 15.2.9. However, Bioeq contested that the JV arrangement violated
Section 16.4, which prohibits transfers of rights or obligations to Third Parties
without prior written consent. Licensee justified the JV by asserting that the arrangement
was not a transfer but rather a subcontracting activity permissible under the agreement.
Additionally, Licensee claimed that even if prior consent was required, the Competitor
Change of Control exempted these activities under the governing provisions.'

evidence_10: The rule describes post-termination obligations regarding transfer of BLA/BLAs, cooperation, and inventory disposition. evidence_3: This rule covers notice of Competitor Change of Control and termination rights. The scenario directly tests this:

  • Licensee sent notice to general customer service email instead of designated legal representative
  • Notice was buried in a broader message
  • This could be a violation of the notice requirement

The rule says "Licensee shall notify Bioeq in writing within [] ([]) days after entry by Licensee into a definitive agreement which would result in a Competitor Change of Control."

The scenario states Licensee "believing it was adhering to the notice requirement, sent a written notification within the required timeframe." However, it was sent to the wrong address and buried in other content. The rule requires written notice but doesn't specify the addressee in detail in the description. System prompt · скрыто по умолчанию

You are a formal verification officer. You verify application packets against a canonical, versioned institutional ruleset. You behave like software: deterministic, grounded, no guessing.

Reply with ONE JSON object and nothing else, exactly this shape:
{
"extracted": {"<canonical field>": <value or null>, ...},
"findings": [
{"rule_id": "<id>", "status": "violation|ok|not_applicable",
"expected": <what the rule requires>, "observed": <what the packet shows>,
"evidence": {"document": "<doc id>", "locator": "<field/sheet/section>", "quote": "<short verbatim quote>"}}
],
"disposition": "accept|needs_correction|reject"
}

Hard requirements:

  • Report EVERY rule of the ruleset exactly once, same rule_ids, in order.
  • Fill every canonical field listed in the task; use null when the packet does not state it. Never invent values.
  • "violation" REQUIRES non-null evidence (document id + locator or verbatim quote) that grounds it in the packet.
  • "evidence" is one object; for cross-document rules use an ARRAY of objects, one per document involved.
  • Use "not_applicable" only when the rule genuinely cannot apply; say why in "observed".
  • disposition: "reject" if any critical violation, "needs_correction" if any non-critical violation, "accept" otherwise.
  • Dates are ISO YYYY-MM-DD; money values are plain numbers without symbols or separators.
  • Emit strictly valid JSON: no trailing commas, no comments, double quotes everywhere.

System prompt · скрыто по умолчанию

You are a formal verification officer. You verify application packets against a canonical, versioned institutional ruleset. You behave like software: deterministic, grounded, no guessing.

Reply with ONE JSON object and nothing else, exactly this shape:
{
"extracted": {"<canonical field>": <value or null>, ...},
"findings": [
{"rule_id": "<id>", "status": "violation|ok|not_applicable",
"expected": <what the rule requires>, "observed": <what the packet shows>,
"evidence": {"document": "<doc id>", "locator": "<field/sheet/section>", "quote": "<short verbatim quote>"}}
],
"disposition": "accept|needs_correction|reject"
}

Hard requirements:

  • Report EVERY rule of the ruleset exactly once, same rule_ids, in order.
  • Fill every canonical field listed in the task; use null when the packet does not state it. Never invent values.
  • "violation" REQUIRES non-null evidence (document id + locator or verbatim quote) that grounds it in the packet.
  • "evidence" is one object; for cross-document rules use an ARRAY of objects, one per document involved.
  • Use "not_applicable" only when the rule genuinely cannot apply; say why in "observed".
  • disposition: "reject" if any critical violation, "needs_correction" if any non-critical violation, "accept" otherwise.
  • Dates are ISO YYYY-MM-DD; money values are plain numbers without symbols or separators.
  • Emit strictly valid JSON: no trailing commas, no comments, double quotes everywhere.

ace_0006❌ ошибка · 1 попыт.
Попытка 1
System prompt · скрыто по умолчанию

You are a formal verification officer. You verify application packets against a canonical, versioned institutional ruleset. You behave like software: deterministic, grounded, no guessing.

Reply with ONE JSON object and nothing else, exactly this shape:
{
"extracted": {"<canonical field>": <value or null>, ...},
"findings": [
{"rule_id": "<id>", "status": "violation|ok|not_applicable",
"expected": <what the rule requires>, "observed": <what the packet shows>,
"evidence": {"document": "<doc id>", "locator": "<field/sheet/section>", "quote": "<short verbatim quote>"}}
],
"disposition": "accept|needs_correction|reject"
}

Hard requirements:

  • Report EVERY rule of the ruleset exactly once, same rule_ids, in order.
  • Fill every canonical field listed in the task; use null when the packet does not state it. Never invent values.
  • "violation" REQUIRES non-null evidence (document id + locator or verbatim quote) that grounds it in the packet.
  • "evidence" is one object; for cross-document rules use an ARRAY of objects, one per document involved.
  • Use "not_applicable" only when the rule genuinely cannot apply; say why in "observed".
  • disposition: "reject" if any critical violation, "needs_correction" if any non-critical violation, "accept" otherwise.
  • Dates are ISO YYYY-MM-DD; money values are plain numbers without symbols or separators.
  • Emit strictly valid JSON: no trailing commas, no comments, double quotes everywhere.
user

SCENARIO UNDER REVIEW:

Company A and Company B enter into a confidentiality agreement regarding the exchange
of sensitive business information, defined as ''Evaluation Material.'' Under the
agreement, Company A is required to delete all Evaluation Material upon termination
of the agreement, except for two specific exceptions outlined in the governing clauses.
After the agreement ends, Company A sends an email to Company B confirming that
all Evaluation Material has been deleted, except for a single copy retained in an
encrypted archive due to automated backup procedures (Condition 1) and another copy
retained to comply with a regulatory requirement to maintain records for seven years
(Condition 2). However, during a routine IT system upgrade, Company A''s IT team
discovers a folder containing Evaluation Material that was inadvertently excluded
from the automated backup system. Believing the material to fall under the ''non-confidential
basis'' exception (Exception evidence_12), the IT team moves the folder to a shared
drive for review and classification. The shared drive is later accessed by a junior
employee who uses the Evaluation Material in a presentation for an unrelated internal
project. The presentation is flagged during an internal audit, and Company B is
informed. Company B accuses Company A of breaching the agreement by failing to delete
the Evaluation Material and improperly accessing it. Company A defends its actions,
stating that the material was excluded from deletion because it was believed to
qualify under the ''non-confidential basis'' exception, as it was obtained from
a third-party vendor who was not bound by confidentiality obligations. Company A
provides documentation showing the vendor''s non-confidential status and argues
that the material was accessed solely for classification purposes during the IT
upgrade.'
</document>

Verify every rule. Reply with the JSON object only.

System prompt · скрыто по умолчанию

You are a formal verification officer. You verify application packets against a canonical, versioned institutional ruleset. You behave like software: deterministic, grounded, no guessing.

Reply with ONE JSON object and nothing else, exactly this shape:
{
"extracted": {"<canonical field>": <value or null>, ...},
"findings": [
{"rule_id": "<id>", "status": "violation|ok|not_applicable",
"expected": <what the rule requires>, "observed": <what the packet shows>,
"evidence": {"document": "<doc id>", "locator": "<field/sheet/section>", "quote": "<short verbatim quote>"}}
],
"disposition": "accept|needs_correction|reject"
}

Hard requirements:

  • Report EVERY rule of the ruleset exactly once, same rule_ids, in order.
  • Fill every canonical field listed in the task; use null when the packet does not state it. Never invent values.
  • "violation" REQUIRES non-null evidence (document id + locator or verbatim quote) that grounds it in the packet.
  • "evidence" is one object; for cross-document rules use an ARRAY of objects, one per document involved.
  • Use "not_applicable" only when the rule genuinely cannot apply; say why in "observed".
  • disposition: "reject" if any critical violation, "needs_correction" if any non-critical violation, "accept" otherwise.
  • Dates are ISO YYYY-MM-DD; money values are plain numbers without symbols or separators.
  • Emit strictly valid JSON: no trailing commas, no comments, double quotes everywhere.

System prompt · скрыто по умолчанию

You are a formal verification officer. You verify application packets against a canonical, versioned institutional ruleset. You behave like software: deterministic, grounded, no guessing.

Reply with ONE JSON object and nothing else, exactly this shape:
{
"extracted": {"<canonical field>": <value or null>, ...},
"findings": [
{"rule_id": "<id>", "status": "violation|ok|not_applicable",
"expected": <what the rule requires>, "observed": <what the packet shows>,
"evidence": {"document": "<doc id>", "locator": "<field/sheet/section>", "quote": "<short verbatim quote>"}}
],
"disposition": "accept|needs_correction|reject"
}

Hard requirements:

  • Report EVERY rule of the ruleset exactly once, same rule_ids, in order.
  • Fill every canonical field listed in the task; use null when the packet does not state it. Never invent values.
  • "violation" REQUIRES non-null evidence (document id + locator or verbatim quote) that grounds it in the packet.
  • "evidence" is one object; for cross-document rules use an ARRAY of objects, one per document involved.
  • Use "not_applicable" only when the rule genuinely cannot apply; say why in "observed".
  • disposition: "reject" if any critical violation, "needs_correction" if any non-critical violation, "accept" otherwise.
  • Dates are ISO YYYY-MM-DD; money values are plain numbers without symbols or separators.
  • Emit strictly valid JSON: no trailing commas, no comments, double quotes everywhere.

System prompt · скрыто по умолчанию

You are a formal verification officer. You verify application packets against a canonical, versioned institutional ruleset. You behave like software: deterministic, grounded, no guessing.

Reply with ONE JSON object and nothing else, exactly this shape:
{
"extracted": {"<canonical field>": <value or null>, ...},
"findings": [
{"rule_id": "<id>", "status": "violation|ok|not_applicable",
"expected": <what the rule requires>, "observed": <what the packet shows>,
"evidence": {"document": "<doc id>", "locator": "<field/sheet/section>", "quote": "<short verbatim quote>"}}
],
"disposition": "accept|needs_correction|reject"
}

Hard requirements:

  • Report EVERY rule of the ruleset exactly once, same rule_ids, in order.
  • Fill every canonical field listed in the task; use null when the packet does not state it. Never invent values.
  • "violation" REQUIRES non-null evidence (document id + locator or verbatim quote) that grounds it in the packet.
  • "evidence" is one object; for cross-document rules use an ARRAY of objects, one per document involved.
  • Use "not_applicable" only when the rule genuinely cannot apply; say why in "observed".
  • disposition: "reject" if any critical violation, "needs_correction" if any non-critical violation, "accept" otherwise.
  • Dates are ISO YYYY-MM-DD; money values are plain numbers without symbols or separators.
  • Emit strictly valid JSON: no trailing commas, no comments, double quotes everywhere.

sublicensing'' only applied to direct sublicensing by Excite@Home itself, not to
sublicensing indirectly facilitated through a JV entity or its subsequent arrangements.
Further complicating the matter, Excite@Home recorded the terms of the sublicensing
arrangement during an informal verbal meeting with the JV partners, instead of formalizing
it in a written communication to the Application Provider. When questioned during

the third-party distributor was operationally independent and outside of Excite@Home''s
direct control. However, internal email records later revealed that Excite@Home
had been informed of the distributor''s ownership by the Named Competitor prior
to approving the JV''s sublicensing arrangement. The Application Provider subsequently

alleging that Excite@Home failed to adhere to the license agreement''s terms and

{"rule_id": "<id>", "status": "violation|ok|not_applicable",

Hard requirements:

  • Report EVERY rule of the ruleset exactly once, same rule_ids, in order.

  • "violation" REQUIRES non-null evidence (document id + locator or verbatim quote) that grounds it in the packet.
  • "evidence" is one object; for cross-document rules use an ARRAY of objects, one per document involved.
  • Use "not_applicable" only when the rule genuinely cannot apply; say why in "observed".
  • disposition: "reject" if any critical violation, "needs_correction" if any non-critical violation, "accept" otherwise.
  • Dates are ISO YYYY-MM-DD; money values are plain numbers without symbols or separators.

However, the scenario raises questions about whether the existing insurance adequately covers Mark. The auditors found that the umbrella policy excluded independent contractors. But this is about the umbrella policy terms, not about the JV's authority under evidence_5.

System prompt · скрыто по умолчанию

You are a formal verification officer. You verify application packets against a canonical, versioned institutional ruleset. You behave like software: deterministic, grounded, no guessing.

Reply with ONE JSON object and nothing else, exactly this shape:
{
"extracted": {"<canonical field>": <value or null>, ...},
"findings": [
{"rule_id": "<id>", "status": "violation|ok|not_applicable",
"expected": <what the rule requires>, "observed": <what the packet shows>,
"evidence": {"document": "<doc id>", "locator": "<field/sheet/section>", "quote": "<short verbatim quote>"}}
],
"disposition": "accept|needs_correction|reject"
}

Hard requirements:

  • Report EVERY rule of the ruleset exactly once, same rule_ids, in order.
  • Fill every canonical field listed in the task; use null when the packet does not state it. Never invent values.
  • "violation" REQUIRES non-null evidence (document id + locator or verbatim quote) that grounds it in the packet.
  • "evidence" is one object; for cross-document rules use an ARRAY of objects, one per document involved.
  • Use "not_applicable" only when the rule genuinely cannot apply; say why in "observed".
  • disposition: "reject" if any critical violation, "needs_correction" if any non-critical violation, "accept" otherwise.
  • Dates are ISO YYYY-MM-DD; money values are plain numbers without symbols or separators.
  • Emit strictly valid JSON: no trailing commas, no comments, double quotes everywhere.

the Customer’s project lead in the relevant message thread. The notice was marked
as ''archived'' by the platform after 24 hours and was not immediately visible without
searching the thread history. Additionally, Philips included a disclaimer in the
message stating that the algorithm remained part of ''Philips Retained Product IP''
and could not be sublicensed or used for non-Product-related purposes. This disclaimer,
while accurate under the agreement, caused confusion within the Customer’s team,
as it seemed to cast doubt on whether the license was actually granted. Despite
these concerns, Philips made no further clarifications, relying on the Customer
to interpret the shared platform message and disclaimer as compliant notice of the
license grant. Later, the Customer raised concerns, alleging that Philips failed
to properly grant the license and questioning the validity of the method used to
communicate the grant.'

upon execution of the Agreement and at all times during the Term. Midway through

herein.'' However, instead of providing a formal certificate of insurance upon policy
execution, EFS supplied a detailed coverage summary from the insurance broker, which
included policy limits, covered entities, and the effective dates. EFS reasoned
that the coverage summary was sufficient to demonstrate compliance, as it contained
all relevant details required for Client to verify the policy''s validity. Additionally,
EFS argued that obtaining a formal certificate of insurance would cause unnecessary
delays in business operations, as the insurer required additional administrative
processing time for certificate issuance. Simultaneously, EFS assured Client that
the certificate would follow within two weeks, after the insurer completed its internal
formalities. While the policy itself was active and there was no gap in coverage,
Client’s compliance officer raised concerns that the governing clauses explicitly
required ''certificates of insurance'' as evidence of coverage and that any alternative
documentation was insufficient. Despite EFS’s efforts to expedite the certificate
delivery, it was ultimately issued and provided to Client 12 days after the new
policy became effective, leaving a critical gap in formal evidence during the interim
period.'

System prompt · скрыто по умолчанию

You are a formal verification officer. You verify application packets against a canonical, versioned institutional ruleset. You behave like software: deterministic, grounded, no guessing.

Reply with ONE JSON object and nothing else, exactly this shape:
{
"extracted": {"<canonical field>": <value or null>, ...},
"findings": [
{"rule_id": "<id>", "status": "violation|ok|not_applicable",
"expected": <what the rule requires>, "observed": <what the packet shows>,
"evidence": {"document": "<doc id>", "locator": "<field/sheet/section>", "quote": "<short verbatim quote>"}}
],
"disposition": "accept|needs_correction|reject"
}

Hard requirements:

  • Report EVERY rule of the ruleset exactly once, same rule_ids, in order.
  • Fill every canonical field listed in the task; use null when the packet does not state it. Never invent values.
  • "violation" REQUIRES non-null evidence (document id + locator or verbatim quote) that grounds it in the packet.
  • "evidence" is one object; for cross-document rules use an ARRAY of objects, one per document involved.
  • Use "not_applicable" only when the rule genuinely cannot apply; say why in "observed".
  • disposition: "reject" if any critical violation, "needs_correction" if any non-critical violation, "accept" otherwise.
  • Dates are ISO YYYY-MM-DD; money values are plain numbers without symbols or separators.
  • Emit strictly valid JSON: no trailing commas, no comments, double quotes everywhere.

In January 2023, Company A, the distributor for Hydraspin, launched an ambitious
marketing campaign targeting businesses in Region Y, outside its designated territory.
This campaign involved email outreach, web banner advertisements, and a virtual

under Hydraspin’s agreement with a third-party distributor, Company C. Hydraspin
had sent a notification email to Company A on December 15, 2022, informing them
of the exclusive arrangement with Company C. However, the email lacked specificity,
vaguely referencing ''Region Y'' without delineating the subregions subject to exclusivity.
Additionally, the email did not include a formal company letterhead, nor was it
marked as a legally binding notice. Believing the email lacked formal weight, Company
A proceeded to host the virtual webinar, inviting businesses across all subregions
of Region Y, including Company Z. The webinar materials did not specifically target
Company Z but included general marketing content that was accessible to all participants.
Following the webinar, Company Z expressed interest in Hydraspin’s products and
initiated contact with Company A for additional information. Upon learning of this,
Company C accused Company A of breaching the exclusivity clause by indirectly soliciting
businesses within its exclusive territory through the webinar. Company A defended
its actions, asserting that the lack of clear geographic boundaries in Hydraspin’s
email made it impossible to determine which subregions were off-limits and that
the webinar was a general marketing effort not aimed at specific businesses.'

System prompt · скрыто по умолчанию

You are a formal verification officer. You verify application packets against a canonical, versioned institutional ruleset. You behave like software: deterministic, grounded, no guessing.

Reply with ONE JSON object and nothing else, exactly this shape:
{
"extracted": {"<canonical field>": <value or null>, ...},
"findings": [
{"rule_id": "<id>", "status": "violation|ok|not_applicable",
"expected": <what the rule requires>, "observed": <what the packet shows>,
"evidence": {"document": "<doc id>", "locator": "<field/sheet/section>", "quote": "<short verbatim quote>"}}
],
"disposition": "accept|needs_correction|reject"
}

Hard requirements:

  • Report EVERY rule of the ruleset exactly once, same rule_ids, in order.
  • Fill every canonical field listed in the task; use null when the packet does not state it. Never invent values.
  • "violation" REQUIRES non-null evidence (document id + locator or verbatim quote) that grounds it in the packet.
  • "evidence" is one object; for cross-document rules use an ARRAY of objects, one per document involved.
  • Use "not_applicable" only when the rule genuinely cannot apply; say why in "observed".
  • disposition: "reject" if any critical violation, "needs_correction" if any non-critical violation, "accept" otherwise.
  • Dates are ISO YYYY-MM-DD; money values are plain numbers without symbols or separators.
  • Emit strictly valid JSON: no trailing commas, no comments, double quotes everywhere.

of what they were required to provide under the agreement. MacroGenics communicated
their compliance through a formal letter, citing their interpretation of the clause,
which was delivered to Green Cross'' legal department. Green Cross, upon testing
the delivered product, alleged non-compliance, maintaining that the proprietary
stabilizing agent was integral to the ''Products'' as it was essential for MGAH22''s

System prompt · скрыто по умолчанию

You are a formal verification officer. You verify application packets against a canonical, versioned institutional ruleset. You behave like software: deterministic, grounded, no guessing.

Reply with ONE JSON object and nothing else, exactly this shape:
{
"extracted": {"<canonical field>": <value or null>, ...},
"findings": [
{"rule_id": "<id>", "status": "violation|ok|not_applicable",
"expected": <what the rule requires>, "observed": <what the packet shows>,
"evidence": {"document": "<doc id>", "locator": "<field/sheet/section>", "quote": "<short verbatim quote>"}}
],
"disposition": "accept|needs_correction|reject"
}

Hard requirements:

  • Report EVERY rule of the ruleset exactly once, same rule_ids, in order.
  • Fill every canonical field listed in the task; use null when the packet does not state it. Never invent values.
  • "violation" REQUIRES non-null evidence (document id + locator or verbatim quote) that grounds it in the packet.
  • "evidence" is one object; for cross-document rules use an ARRAY of objects, one per document involved.
  • Use "not_applicable" only when the rule genuinely cannot apply; say why in "observed".
  • disposition: "reject" if any critical violation, "needs_correction" if any non-critical violation, "accept" otherwise.
  • Dates are ISO YYYY-MM-DD; money values are plain numbers without symbols or separators.
  • Emit strictly valid JSON: no trailing commas, no comments, double quotes everywhere.

System prompt · скрыто по умолчанию

You are a formal verification officer. You verify application packets against a canonical, versioned institutional ruleset. You behave like software: deterministic, grounded, no guessing.

Reply with ONE JSON object and nothing else, exactly this shape:
{
"extracted": {"<canonical field>": <value or null>, ...},
"findings": [
{"rule_id": "<id>", "status": "violation|ok|not_applicable",
"expected": <what the rule requires>, "observed": <what the packet shows>,
"evidence": {"document": "<doc id>", "locator": "<field/sheet/section>", "quote": "<short verbatim quote>"}}
],
"disposition": "accept|needs_correction|reject"
}

Hard requirements:

  • Report EVERY rule of the ruleset exactly once, same rule_ids, in order.
  • Fill every canonical field listed in the task; use null when the packet does not state it. Never invent values.
  • "violation" REQUIRES non-null evidence (document id + locator or verbatim quote) that grounds it in the packet.
  • "evidence" is one object; for cross-document rules use an ARRAY of objects, one per document involved.
  • Use "not_applicable" only when the rule genuinely cannot apply; say why in "observed".
  • disposition: "reject" if any critical violation, "needs_correction" if any non-critical violation, "accept" otherwise.
  • Dates are ISO YYYY-MM-DD; money values are plain numbers without symbols or separators.
  • Emit strictly valid JSON: no trailing commas, no comments, double quotes everywhere.

System prompt · скрыто по умолчанию

You are a formal verification officer. You verify application packets against a canonical, versioned institutional ruleset. You behave like software: deterministic, grounded, no guessing.

Reply with ONE JSON object and nothing else, exactly this shape:
{
"extracted": {"<canonical field>": <value or null>, ...},
"findings": [
{"rule_id": "<id>", "status": "violation|ok|not_applicable",
"expected": <what the rule requires>, "observed": <what the packet shows>,
"evidence": {"document": "<doc id>", "locator": "<field/sheet/section>", "quote": "<short verbatim quote>"}}
],
"disposition": "accept|needs_correction|reject"
}

Hard requirements:

  • Report EVERY rule of the ruleset exactly once, same rule_ids, in order.
  • Fill every canonical field listed in the task; use null when the packet does not state it. Never invent values.
  • "violation" REQUIRES non-null evidence (document id + locator or verbatim quote) that grounds it in the packet.
  • "evidence" is one object; for cross-document rules use an ARRAY of objects, one per document involved.
  • Use "not_applicable" only when the rule genuinely cannot apply; say why in "observed".
  • disposition: "reject" if any critical violation, "needs_correction" if any non-critical violation, "accept" otherwise.
  • Dates are ISO YYYY-MM-DD; money values are plain numbers without symbols or separators.
  • Emit strictly valid JSON: no trailing commas, no comments, double quotes everywhere.

System prompt · скрыто по умолчанию

You are a formal verification officer. You verify application packets against a canonical, versioned institutional ruleset. You behave like software: deterministic, grounded, no guessing.

Reply with ONE JSON object and nothing else, exactly this shape:
{
"extracted": {"<canonical field>": <value or null>, ...},
"findings": [
{"rule_id": "<id>", "status": "violation|ok|not_applicable",
"expected": <what the rule requires>, "observed": <what the packet shows>,
"evidence": {"document": "<doc id>", "locator": "<field/sheet/section>", "quote": "<short verbatim quote>"}}
],
"disposition": "accept|needs_correction|reject"
}

Hard requirements:

  • Report EVERY rule of the ruleset exactly once, same rule_ids, in order.
  • Fill every canonical field listed in the task; use null when the packet does not state it. Never invent values.
  • "violation" REQUIRES non-null evidence (document id + locator or verbatim quote) that grounds it in the packet.
  • "evidence" is one object; for cross-document rules use an ARRAY of objects, one per document involved.
  • Use "not_applicable" only when the rule genuinely cannot apply; say why in "observed".
  • disposition: "reject" if any critical violation, "needs_correction" if any non-critical violation, "accept" otherwise.
  • Dates are ISO YYYY-MM-DD; money values are plain numbers without symbols or separators.
  • Emit strictly valid JSON: no trailing commas, no comments, double quotes everywhere.
